Sure Convergence and Constructive Universal Approximation for Multi-Layer Neural Networks
Chien-Ming Chi

Abstract
We propose a new neural network model, 01Neuro, built on indicator activation neurons. Its boosted variant possesses two key statistical properties: (1) Sure Convergence, where model optimization can be achieved with high probability given sufficient computational resources; and (2) Constructive Universal Approximation: In the infinite-sample setting, the model can approximate any finite sum of measurable functions, each depending on only k k out of p p input features, provided the architecture is properly tuned. Unlike most universal approximation results that are agnostic to training procedures, our guarantees are directly tied to the model’s explicit construction and optimization algorithm. To improve prediction stability, we integrate stochastic training and bagging into the boosted 01Neuro framework. Empirical evaluations on simulated and real-world tabular datasets with small to medium sample sizes highlight its strengths: effective approximation of interaction components (multiplicative terms), stable prediction performance (comparable to Random Forests), robustness to many noisy features ( p = 100 p=100 , n ≤ 450 n\leq 450 ), and insensitivity to feature scaling. A major limitation of the current implementation of boosted 01Neuro is its higher computational cost, which is approximately 5 to 30 times that of Random Forests and XGBoost.



Running title
: Sure Convergence and Constructive Universal Approximation

Keywords
: Boosted greedy approximation; sparse neural networks; exploit–explore optimization; active and idle neurons; nonconvex optimization problems.

1
Introduction

Predictive models based on neural networks
[
35
]
lie at the core of recent artificial intelligence advancements, including AlphaGo
[
48
]
, GPT-3
[
9
]
, and AlphaFold
[
33
]
. Their high prediction accuracy has been extensively studied and supported from theoretical perspectives, spanning from approximation results for single-hidden-layer networks
[
30
,
14
,
4
]
to advanced universal approximation theories for multilayer networks
[
6
,
45
]
.

However, despite their strong empirical performance and universal approximation ability, neural networks can sometimes fall short in practice. Numerous formal studies have shown that neural networks may not outperform other prediction methods on tabular data
[
23
,
40
,
46
]
. In particular, multilayer neural networks have been found to be consistently outperformed by tree-based models
[
23
]
, a result that appears to conflict with their universal approximation capabilities. This gap between theory and practice has been a subject of extensive investigation
[
1
,
24
]
. A plausible explanation for this gap may be the failure to achieve complete model optimization, a condition that universal approximation theory assumes
[
45
,
6
]
but is rarely guaranteed in practice
[
18
]
.
Whether neural networks inherently possess universal approximation capability by construction, often called constructive approximation
[
20
]
, or how this ability might be effectively realized, remains a significant open problem.

At least three main approaches have been pursued to address this problem. First, adaptive gradient methods such as Adam and other variants
[
34
,
28
,
16
]
have been proposed and recently shown to converge under certain conditions
[
55
,
36
,
15
]
. Nevertheless, both theoretical
[
32
]
and empirical
[
12
]
studies indicate that gradient-based optimization often converges to local minima for which universal approximation is not guaranteed. Second, from the perspectives of neural tangent kernels
[
31
,
54
]
or mean field theory
[
42
,
41
]
, sufficiently wide and often impractically large neural networks can be optimized using gradient methods. However, several key theoretical assumptions limit the practical applicability of these results for understanding neural network optimization. The third approach attempts to develop constructive approximation theory for neural networks
[
20
,
2
]
. While gradient-based update methods and network architectures have been appropriately considered, these related theories are still in their early stages, with current results limited to single-hidden-layer or shallow networks.

This paper makes a promising contribution to the constructive approximation of neural networks by developing a new network model called 01Neuro. The new network utilizes indicator activation functions instead of standard smooth ones, adopts a sparse structure with both active and idle neurons, and is trained using Exploit-Explore Optimizing, a newly proposed method that iteratively updates its weights and biases. When the width of 01Neuro is properly tuned, the network includes a sufficient number of idle neurons, which do not directly influence the output but allow the model to explore alternative subnetworks. This helps eliminate the risk of becoming trapped in local minima during optimization. We formally prove the sure convergence property of the boosted variant
[
19
]
of 01Neuro, demonstrating that its weight and bias optimization through Exploit-Explore Optimizing can be practically completed in a probabilistic sense, given sufficient computational resources. In other words, the proposed model offers a principled solution to nonconvex optimization in network training. We further establish constructive universal approximation for the proposed model, showing that boosted 01Neuro can approximate any finite sum of measurable functions, each depending on
k
out of
p
coordinates, with sufficient network depth (
L\geq 2+\lceil\log_{2}k\rceil
) and an appropriate level of sparsity.

We empirically demonstrate the universal approximation capability of the proposed model in Section
6
using small-scale simulated data with numerous noisy features. In Section
7
, we further compare boosted 01Neuro with two popular tree-based models, XGBoost
[
11
]
and Random Forests
[
8
]
, to evaluate their prediction performance
[
22
,
40
]
. The version of boosted 01Neuro evaluated in these two sections is trained with the practical training techniques presented in Section
5
. Our empirical results suggest that the proposed model offers four practical advantages: effective approximation of complex components (multiplicative terms), stable prediction performance (comparable to Random Forests), robustness to numerous noisy features (
p=100
,
n\leq 450
), and insensitivity to feature scaling. One potential drawback of the current implementation is its comparatively high computational cost, which is approximately 5 to 30 times greater than that of the slower of Random Forests and XGBoost.

Our proposed model may be particularly appealing, for example, to researchers aiming to develop constructive and well-grounded neural network inference methods, and to practitioners looking to develop neural network–based feature representation techniques for applications with limited sample sizes or irregular input features.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Section
2
introduces the architecture of boosted 01Neuro, while Section
3
presents Exploit-Explore Optimizing and its sure convergence property. In Section
4
, we establish the constructive universal approximation theory for the proposed model.
All technical proofs are deferred to the online Supplementary Material.

2
Sparse Neural Networks with Indicator Neurons

The neural network considered in this paper, 01Neuro, maps
p
-dimensional input features to a
q
-dimensional output. The name 01Neuro reflects the use of indicator activation functions, which produce binary feature representations throughout the network’s forward pass. The architecture of an 01Neuro consists of
L-1
hidden layers and an output layer. Each hidden layer
l\in\{1,\cdots,L-1\}
contains
p_{l}
neurons, and the output layer consists of a single neuron with
p_{L}=1
. For simplicity, in this paper, we consider the applications with
q=1
.

h\in\{1,\cdots,p\}

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}_{l,h}\right\rVert_{0}\leq w_{0}
for each
l\in\{1,\cdots,L\}
and each
h\in\{1,\cdots,p_{l}\}
, with some integer parameter
w_{0}>0
. In our sparse networks, many neuron functions (also called neuron units or simply neurons) do not contribute to the final output
f_{L}
and are referred to as idle neurons, while those that do affect the output are called active neurons. Section
3
explains how weight exploration is guided by the utilization of idle neurons. Figure
1
illustrates an example of a 01Neuro with calibrated parameters, as listed in Table
6
of Section
A.1
, that approximates a diamond-shaped region.

Figure 1
:
An example of 01Neuro with
L=3,p=3,p_{1}=8,p_{2}=4,p_{3}=1
, and
w_{0}=2
. In the left panel, dashed lines indicate inactive neuron functions, while solid lines indicate active ones. The right panel illustrates how 01Neuro progressively transforms the 3-dimensional input features
\vec{x}=(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})^{{}^{T}}
into a diamond-shaped output function on the
x_{1}
–
x_{2}
plane. The red region indicates where an indicator neuron function
f_{l,h}
takes the value one.

2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}).

(3)

Section
3
demonstrates that our proposed model updating approach, called Exploit-Explore Optimizing, can solve the nonconvex optimization problem in (
2
) and achieve sure convergence, meaning the optimization reliably converges in a probabilistic sense, given sufficient computational resources. On the other hand, Example
1
in Section
3.2
and Table
1
in Section
5.4
offer recommended practical choices for 01Neuro architectures.

3
Sure Convergence via Exploit-Explore Optimizing

3.1
Exploit-Explore Optimizing

In this section, we introduce the Exploit-Explore Optimizing for solving the nonconvex optimization problem defined in (
2
). The method updates a network function iteratively, aiming to eventually reach an optimal configuration. Specifically, consider a generic training sample
\{U_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
, a network function

(4)

In what follows, we describe how Exploit-Explore Optimizing updates the network function
g
in a single round. As a remark, to solve the optimization problem in (
2
), we set
U_{i}=m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})
and
F=F_{s}
and apply Exploit-Explore Optimizing over multiple update rounds for each
s\in\{1,\cdots,M\}
.

In each round, the neuron units of
g
are updated sequentially, starting from the output layer and moving toward the input layer. At the
l
th layer, each unit
h\in\{1,\cdots,p_{l}\}
is updated in order. Specifically, for the
(l,h)
th neuron with
l\in\{1,\cdots,L\}
, we update its weight vector and bias
(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern as follows.

is replaced by
(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle u\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle u\hfil$\crcr}}}_{k},b_{k})
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,K\}
. Additionally,
g_{K+1}=g
is included to ensure that the fitted loss does not increase. Now, the current network output function
g
is updated to new function
g^{\text{new}}
, defined as:

\begin{split}g^{\text{new}}&=\argmin_{g\in\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{K+1}\}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(U_{i}-F(g,\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)^{2},\end{split}

(5)

where
F
is given by (
4
). Here, ties are broken by selecting the
g_{i}
with the higher index.

After all neural units in the model have been updated in a round, the procedure proceeds to the next round of updating.

3.1.1
Training Complexity

In each update round, optimization of the form (
5
) is performed only on active neurons, with their total number bounded by
\sum_{l=1}^{L}w_{0}^{L-l}
, where at most
w_{0}^{L-l}
neurons are active in the
l
th layer. This holds regardless of the number of idle neurons (see Figure
1
and the remark before Condition
1
in Section
3.2
for definitions of active and idle neurons). For idle neurons not connected to the output,
g^{\text{new}}
is sampled uniformly from
\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{K+1}\}
to reduce computational cost. Although idle neurons do not directly affect the output, they still contribute to the training complexity. In particular, Section
A.3
shows that the training complexity of boosted 01Neuro over a single update round is approximately linear in
nMK\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L}p_{l}p_{l-1}\right)\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L}w_{0}^{L-l}\right)
when
n
is large. This result can be used to estimate the training time required for the recommended 01Neuro architectures presented in Table
1
of Section
5.4
.

For comparison, training a single 01Neuro model (without boosting) with
b=1
update round requires computational time comparable to that of training a fully grown decision tree
[
8
]
, given the 01Neuro architectures listed in Table
1
. In our experiments reported in Sections
6
–
7
, a boosted 01Neuro model with
M\in\{3,4,\cdots,50\}
, using bagging (see Section
5
), typically requires 5 to 30 times more computational time than the slower of Random Forests or XGBoost. Detailed runtime statistics for real data experiments are provided in Table
5
of Section
7
.

On the other hand, idle neurons play a critical role in enabling effective optimization, as discussed in Sections
3.1.2
–
3.2
. The pyramid-shaped architecture suggested in Example
1
strikes a practical balance between active and idle neurons at each layer.

3.1.2
Optimization Dynamics

Exploit-Explore Optimizing in (
5
) provides effective optimization dynamics and prevents convergence to local optima by enabling continued exploration of alternative network structures through idle neurons. For example, in Figure
1
, if all active neurons
f_{2,1}
and
f_{2,2}
at the second layer becomes stuck in a suboptimal configuration, an idle neuron such as
f_{2,4}
can explore other combinations—recall that applying (
5
) to an idle neuron is equivalent to randomly selecting
g^{\text{new}}
from
{g_{1},\cdots,g_{K+1}}
. An idle neuron may randomly select a promising subnetwork, becoming active in subsequent rounds as higher layers are updated (e.g., for
f_{3}
). The sparse architecture defined in Condition
1
ensures such exploration remains possible at every layer, supporting 01Neuro’s sure convergence property, which is formally stated in Theorem
1
. It is worth noting that having many idle neurons increases the chances of discovering better network structures during optimization. However, as discussed in Section
3.1.1
, idle neurons still consume computational resources, even though they are not actively optimized.

3.2
Sure Convergence

Gradient-based methods are not directly applicable to indicator activation functions because the gradients of such networks are zero almost everywhere
[
37
]
, making optimization particularly challenging due to the absence of informative updates. In contrast, Exploit-Explore Optimizing takes advantage of this zero-gradient property to optimize weights and biases effectively. The key idea is that the indifferent region (the zero-gradient region) surrounding the optimal weights and bias of 01Neuro is sufficiently large that a randomly sampled pair of weights and bias falls within it with positive probability, thereby motivating the design of Exploit-Explore Optimizing. This intuition is illustrated in Figure
2
, where pairs of weights and biases from the indifferent region separate the five samples into the same two subsamples (black and gray).

Figure 2
:
An example with five input vectors
\vec{v}_{i}\in[0,1]^{2}
. With positive probability, the Exploit-Explore Optimizing algorithm may sample a weight–bias pair
(\vec{w},\vec{w}^{{}^{T}}\vec{v}_{1})
that produces the same partition of black and gray points as the pair shown in the left panel. The pair
(\vec{w},\vec{w}^{{}^{T}}\vec{v}_{1})
defines a hyperplane whose intersection with the
x_{1}
–
x_{2}
plane has a slope lying between those defined by
\vec{w}_{+}
and
\vec{w}_{-}
, as indicated by the circular arrows in the right panel. The red arrows mark the regions where
\vec{w}_{+}^{{}^{T}}\vec{x}>\vec{w}_{+}^{{}^{T}}\vec{v}_{1}
and
\vec{w}_{-}^{{}^{T}}\vec{x}>\vec{w}_{-}^{{}^{T}}\vec{v}_{1}
. Importantly, this property applies to any weight–bias pair, including the optimal pair that minimizes the sample
L_{2}
loss, and is not limited to the specific example shown on the left.

1
formally establishes that for any input set
\mathcal{N}
consisting of elements from
[0,1]^{k}
and any pair

below.

1
guarantees sufficient idle neuron units for exploring network structures. A practical 01Neuro structure satisfying this condition is presented in Example
1
below. In Theorem
1
, the initial 01Neuro model is denoted by
g^{(0)}\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
, while
g^{(b)}\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
denotes the model after
b
rounds of Exploit-Explore Optimizing.

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})>b_{2}\}
are disjoint.

Theorem 1

(Sure Convergence)
.

Let a sample
\mathcal{X}_{n}=\{(U_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be given, along with a 01Neuro architecture satisfying Condition
1
, an initial model
g^{(0)}\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
, and
F
defined by (
4
). Apply Exploit-Explore Optimizing for
b
rounds, and obtain
g^{(b)}\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
. Then,
\lim_{b\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(U_{i}-F(g^{(b)},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)^{2}=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(U_{i}-F(g,\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)^{2}\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}\big)=1
.

The proof of Theorem
1
is deferred to the online Supplementary Material.
Theorem
1
ensures that the probability of achieving complete optimization can be made arbitrarily high, provided sufficient computational resources, measured by the number of update rounds
b
. This theorem establishes the sure convergence property for the update rule in (
5
), under a general training sample and function
F
. To solve the optimization problem in (
2
), Exploit-Explore Optimizing can be applied in two ways: either by updating each boosted 01Neuro predictor over multiple rounds (mentioned in Section
3.1
), or by updating all predictors in sequence within each round and repeating this process for multiple rounds (introduced in Section
5.1
). When
M
is finite, the same arguments as in Theorem
1
apply directly (with details omitted for brevity) to show that convergence holds for both approaches, thereby ensuring the sure convergence of the boosted 01Neuro model in (
2
).

Example
1
below summarizes all hyperparameters for our boosted 01Neuro model and its network architecture. Given the sparsity parameter
w_{0}
, the maximum number of active neuron units at the
l
th layer is
w_{0}^{L-l}
for
l\in\{1,\cdots,L-1\}
. Therefore, if
p_{l}\geq 2w_{0}^{L-l}
, indicating that there are at least as many idle neurons as active ones at each layer, then Condition
1
is satisfied. The detailed proof of this result is omitted. Meanwhile, Table
1
in Section
5.4
presents the hyperparameter space of 01Neuro used for model tuning.

Example 1
.

A boosted 01Neuro model is governed by the following hyperparameters: the number of sampled candidate sets per optimization step
K
in (
5
), number of update rounds
b
, sparsity level
w_{0}
, and layer-wise neuron widths
p_{l}\geq 2w_{0}^{L-l}
for
l\in\{1,\cdots,L-1\}
with
p_{L}=1
. This 01Neuro architecture satisfies Condition
1
. Additionally, there are boosting learning rate
\gamma\in(0,1]
and the number of boosting iterations
M
.

3.3
Review of Optimization Convergence in Neural Networks

Training neural networks typically involves minimizing the empirical
L_{2}
-loss, as defined in (
2
), over a given class of network functions. Among the most widely adopted training algorithms are adaptive gradient methods such as Adam and its variants
[
34
,
28
,
16
]
, which have recently been shown to converge under certain conditions
[
55
,
36
,
15
]
. Nevertheless, both theoretical
[
32
]
and empirical
[
12
]
studies have demonstrated that gradient-based optimization tends to converge to local minima. While many local minima in sufficiently large or overparameterized networks yield good generalization performance
[
12
]
, significant challenges remain due to the gap between theoretical guarantees and the practical behavior of neural networks
[
1
,
24
]
.

Complementary to this, a line of work has investigated the convergence and generalization properties of infinitely wide neural networks trained via gradient descent
[
31
,
10
,
3
]
, or adaptive methods such as Adam
[
38
]
, under the framework of the Neural Tangent Kernel (NTK). In parallel,
[
42
,
41
]
offer a mean field perspective on the distributional dynamics of model parameters updated by stochastic gradient descent in overparameterized single-hidden-layer networks, analyzing the conditions under which local or global optima may be attained. While the NTK and mean field approaches have significantly advanced the theoretical understanding of optimization in neural networks, their reliance on assumptions such as infinite or very large network width still limits their direct applicability to practical architectures with moderate width used in real-world applications.

In summary, achieving reliable and interpretable optimization in neural network training remains a central open problem. The sure convergence property Theorem
1
contributes a promising step toward addressing this challenge.

4
Universal Approximation of Boosted 01Neuro

In this section, we establish the universal approximation property of the boosted 01Neuro of (
2
). In light of Theorem
1
, our approximation framework based on (
2
) is constructive
[
20
]
, or algorithm-dependent
[
18
]
, in that it accounts for the network’s training procedure. In particular, it draws on principles from greedy approximation theory
[
52
]
to guide the selection of weak predictors during the boosting process
[
19
]
, with each selected predictor optimized in a probabilistic sense as described in Theorem
1
.

The universal approximation theory is developed in the population setting, corresponding to the case of an infinite training sample. At the population level, the response variable is modeled as
Y=m(\boldsymbol{X})+\varepsilon
, where
\boldsymbol{X}
is a
p
-dimensional input feature vector and
\varepsilon
is an independent, zero-mean error term. An independent and identically distributed training sample of size
n
is denoted by
\{(Y_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
. The infinite-sample setting refers to the learning scenario of (
2
) in which, for
l\in\{0,1\}
, every
s>0
, and each
g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
, the sample moments

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\times\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=l\}
and
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=l\}

are replaced by their population counterparts

\mathbb{E}\big(m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X})\times\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X})=l\}\big)
and
\mathbb{P}\big(g(\boldsymbol{X})=l\big),

respectively. Similarly, the update

m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})-\gamma F_{s}(g_{s},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\},

is replaced by

m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})=m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X})-\gamma F_{s}(g_{s},\boldsymbol{X}),

and the initial values
m_{0}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\coloneqq Y_{i}
are replaced by
m_{0}(\boldsymbol{X})\coloneqq m(\boldsymbol{X})
. Lemma
2
below formally derives
\widehat{m}_{M}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern ),

and
\mathcal{P}(g,f(\boldsymbol{X}))\coloneqq\sum_{l=0}^{1}[\mathbb{E}(\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X})=l\}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}}f(\boldsymbol{X}))]^{2}
for every measurable

and each

.

Population-level boosted 01Neuro obtained via (
8
) with
t<1
corresponds to the weak approximation algorithm
[
52
]
, which accounts for practical scenarios where ideal approximation is hindered by estimation variance. Although our analysis focuses on the population setting where estimation variance is absent, we still consider
t\in(0,1]
in the approximation theory for generality.

4.1
Constructive Universal Approximation

Under the infinite-sample setting as in Lemma
2
, we investigate how the boosted 01Neuro approximate the underlying data-generating function, denoted by
m(\boldsymbol{X})
. In the same spirit as illustrated by Figure
1
, a 01Neuro model can approximate any
k
-sparse rectangular indicator function. Consequently, the boosted 01Neuro function class, denoted by
\mathcal{D}
below, is capable of approximating any finite sum of
k
-sparse measurable functions. This result is formally established in Lemma
3
.

\begin{split}\mathcal{D}=&\{\sum_{i=1}^{q}a_{i}g_{i}:g_{i}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g(\boldsymbol{X})=1)}},\frac{1-g(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g(\boldsymbol{X})=0)}}\},g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}},a_{i}\in\mathbb{R},q\in\mathbb{N}\},\end{split}

(9)

where
\mathbb{N}
denote the set of natural numbers. In particular, the proof of Lemma
3
shows that
\mathcal{D}
contains all step functions defined on
[0,1]^{p}
that depend on
k
coordinates in the
p
-dimensional ambient space, provided
L\geq 2+\lceil\log_{2}k\rceil
and
w_{0}\geq 2
. Here, a step function is defined as a linear combinations of finitely many rectangular indicator functions
[
50
]
. Note that the results of Lemma
3
hold regardless of the training method used for 01Neuro.

Condition 2
.

The distribution of
\boldsymbol{X}
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure and admits a probability density function that is bounded from above.

Lemma 3
.

Assume Condition
2
. If
w_{0}\geq 2
and
L\geq 2+\lceil\log_{2}k\rceil
, then
\mathcal{L}(k,R_{0})\subset\overline{\mathcal{D}}
for each integer
R_{0}>0
, where
\mathcal{L}(k,R_{0})=\{\sum_{r=1}^{R_{0}}m^{(r)}:m^{(r)}\in\mathcal{L}(k)\}
, and that

\begin{split}\overline{\mathcal{D}}=&\{m:\text{ Every measurable }m:[0,1]^{p}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\text{ with }\mathbb{E}(m(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}<\infty\\
&\text{ and }\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}(m(\boldsymbol{X})-f_{i}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}=0\text{ for some }f_{i}\in\mathcal{D}\},\\
\mathcal{L}(k)=&\{m:\text{Every measurable }m:[0,1]^{p}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\text{ with }\mathbb{E}[m(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}<\infty\text{ and }\\
&\quad\mathbb{E}(|m(\boldsymbol{X})-m^{\dagger}(X_{i_{1}},\cdots,X_{i_{k}})|)=0\textnormal{ for some }m^{\dagger}:[0,1]^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\\
&\quad\textnormal{ and some }\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\}\subset\{1,\cdots,p\}\}.\end{split}

Based on the results of Lemma
3
, which highlight the potential of boosted 01Neuro without considering the training procedure, we further show in Theorem
2
that the population-level boosted 01Neuro defined in (
7
) is a constructive universal approximator for any function that is a sum of measurable functions, each depending on possibly different sets of
k
coordinates.
Here,
R_{0}
denotes an arbitrary fixed number of additive measurable components in the data-generating function.

Theorem 2
.

Assume Condition
2
. Let
w_{0}\geq 2
,
L\geq 2+\lceil\log_{2}k\rceil
,
m\in\mathcal{L}(k,R_{0})
, and
\widehat{m}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})
as in (
7
) be given, with
t\in(0,1]
and
\gamma\in(0,1]
. Then
\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[\widehat{m}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})-m(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}=0
.

The proof of Theorem
2
is deferred to the online Supplementary Material. The consistency of general boosting methods has been studied by
[
7
]
, while the approximation properties of weak greedy algorithms, which are closely related to our analysis of the boosting algorithm in Theorem
2
, have been investigated by
[
52
]
. However, Theorem
2
focuses on their universal approximation capability, a topic not addressed by
[
7
]
or
[
52
]
. More related works to Theorem
2
are reviewed in Section
4.2
.

The results and arguments for Theorems
1
–
2
provide a comprehensive understanding of how network depth enhances the approximation capability of 01Neuro through boosting, and how network width, combined with the sparse structure of 01Neuro, facilitates sure convergence in training. Our findings on the role of depth align with existing literature showing that deeper networks can achieve more efficient approximation
[
17
,
51
]
. Note that Theorem
2
does not quantify the effects of
t<1
and
\gamma<1
on the approximation error, as convergence rates are not established in this result.

4.2
Discussion

The universal approximation theory for single-hidden-layer neural networks was initially established by
[
30
]
and
[
14
]
. Dimension-free approximation convergence rates for such networks were first provided by
[
4
]
. Extensions of these results to multilayer neural networks have been developed by
[
6
]
and
[
45
]
. These theoretical results focus on the expressive power of neural network function classes, without addressing how such functions can be computed in practice, a framework often referred to as algorithm-independent control
[
18
,
20
]
. For a formal discussion of the gap between approximation theory and practical implementation in neural networks, see
[
1
,
24
]
.

On the other hand, constructive approximation methods, which establish approximation guarantees in conjunction with the training procedure, have been extensively studied using greedy algorithms
[
27
,
47
,
5
]
. In particular,
Barron et al. 2008
propose a greedy algorithm that sequentially selects neuron units in a single-hidden-layer network, resulting in a universal approximator. However, their results are limited to shallow architectures and do not extend to deeper networks, which are central to modern deep learning due to their ability to learn hierarchical feature representations. Additionally,
Barron et al. 2008
require regularization of the function class complexity to apply the greedy algorithm, while the theoretical guarantees assume optimization over the full function class. This suggests that the resulting approximator is not fully constructive.

In contrast, Theorems
1
–
2
collectively demonstrate that our boosted 01Neuro acts as a constructive universal approximator. Particularly, 01Neuro provides a practical solution to nonconvex optimization problems, making it well-suited for real-world applications. This is particularly valuable in field studies that require task-specific representation learning, where the underlying optimization problems are typically nonconvex in nature.

5
Stable Training and Bagging for 01Neuro

In the previous section, we introduced the vanilla version of boosted 01Neuro alongside its solid statistical properties. Starting from this section, we shift our focus toward practical training techniques that are designed to perform well in practice. The evaluation of these methods is primarily based on empirical results, as demonstrated by the data experiments in Sections
6
–
7
.

5.1
Training a Sequence of Boosted 01Neuros

In practice, a single update round for
\widehat{m}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})
(with notation as in (
2
)–(
3
)) is performed through the following steps. (1) Given the training sample
\{(Y_{i},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, we first update the first 01Neuro predictor for a single round and compute the residuals
m_{1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})
. (2) Using
\{(m_{1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}),\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
as training data, we then update the second 01Neuro predictor for a single round and compute the residuals
m_{2}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})
. (3) We proceed in a similar manner to update each subsequent 01Neuro predictor until all
M
predictors have been updated. This training scheme may be repeated for a total of
b
rounds to produce the final boosted 01Neuro predictor
\widehat{m}_{M}(\boldsymbol{X})
. Note that at the beginning of each round, the model’s initial parameters are set to those obtained at the end of the previous round.

5.2
Stochastic and Stabilized Exploit-Explore Optimizing

Each optimization step in (
5
) is performed using a randomly selected subset of the training data, comprising a proportion defined by the hyperparameter
stochastic_ratio

\in[0.8,1]
. That is,
\textsf{stochastic\_ratio}\times\text{(training sample size)}
examples are randomly sampled without replacement for each optimization. Subsampling helps stabilize optimization in finite-sample settings, similar to how stochastic gradient descent
[
44
]
improves upon standard gradient descent. Additionally, in (
5
), we subtract
\textsf{stabilizer}\times n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(U_{i}-\bar{U})^{2}
, where
\bar{U}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
and
\textsf{stabilizer}\in\{0.5,0.1,0.01,0\}
is a hyperparameter, from the objective associated with the current-stage candidate parameter
g_{K+1}
to enhance the stability of the optimized network model.

. By default, we set
N_{\text{bagging}}=20
and use 10 rounds of fine-tuning. As we will see in Section
6
, applying bagging to boosted 01Neuro leads to significant improvements in prediction accuracy.

5.4
Training Schedule

In this section, we summarize the training procedure for the boosted 01Neuro model used in our experiments. Given a dataset for model training, we optimize hyperparameters by uniformly sampling configurations from the space defined in Table
1
. In this table, the
k
th architecture for
k\in\{A,\cdots,F\}
corresponds to the layer configuration
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4},p_{5})
, given by

(4,1,\emptyset,\emptyset,\emptyset)
,

(8,4,1,\emptyset,\emptyset)
,

(16,8,4,1,\emptyset)
,

(4,4,1,\emptyset,\emptyset)
,

(4,4,4,1,\emptyset)
, and

(4,4,4,4,1)
,
where
\emptyset
denotes the absence of a layer at the corresponding position. Note that while not all network architectures considered here satisfy Condition
1
, they are adequate for the purposes of our data experiments.

For each configuration, we train a single boosted 01Neuro model using the methods described in Sections
5.1
–
5.2
, and evaluate its performance on a validation set. During validation, we train the network for
b=20
rounds. Then, we apply 10 bagging predictors, each with one fine-tuning round. This optimization procedure is independently repeated
R\in\{30,100\}
times, each time splitting the data into 80% for training and 20% for validation. Details of the hyperparameter tuning process are provided in Section
A.2
.

After hyperparameter optimization, we retrain the predictive model using the full dataset for
b=120
rounds. We then apply bagging to generated 20 bagging boosted 01Neuro models, each undergoing 10 fine-tuning rounds, to form the final predictive model. If model evaluation is needed, as in Section
7
, a separate test set should be set aside from the dataset prior to hyperparameter tuning, typically by sample splitting. When using a training, validation, and test split, the final predictive model is trained on the combined training and validation sets. To avoid confusion, we refer to this combined set as the full training set.

Parameter space

#
boosting predictors)

\textnormal{Uniform}\{3,4,\cdots,50\}

\gamma
(learning rate)

positive real number

Uniform
[0.05,0.9]

Uniform
[0.8,1]

:

w_{0}=2
.
The number of candidate hyperparameter sets per optimization is
K=10
(see Section
3.1
). For details on the network architecture, see Section
5.4
.

6
Empirical Evaluation of Prediction Capability

In this section, we evaluation the prediction capability of the boosted 01Neuro from Section
5.4
by simulating datasets from the following two data-generating models.

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{10}X_{j}+1.8\times\varepsilon,

(10)

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=2\times X_{1}\times X_{2}+0.5\times X_{3}+0.3\times\varepsilon,

(11)

where
X
is drawn from the uniform distribution over the hypercube
[-1,1]^{p}
, and the independent model error
\varepsilon
follows the standard normal distribution. In our data experiments, we simulate
n\in\{150,450\}
i.i.d. samples from each of Models (
10
)–(
11
), with
p=100
. The signal-to-noise ratio in the first case is approximately 1:1, while the variances of
2\times X_{1}\times X_{2}
,
0.5\times X_{3}
, and
0.3\times\varepsilon
are in the approximate ratio 5:1:1. With Models (
10
)–(
11
), we aim to evaluate the predictive models’ ability to handle noisy features with noisy errors, numerous additive components, and challenging scenarios such as complex multiplicative interactions. Given the limited sample size, we focus on these settings where model performance does not plateau, enabling a more meaningful comparison of stability and generalization across methods.

The performance of boosted 01Neuro with bagging (referred to as bagging boosted 01Neuro) is evaluated using R
2
scores and compared to its counterpart without bagging (single boosted 01Neuro), as well as to two popular tree-based models: XGBoost
[
11
]
and Random Forests
[
8
]
. These two serve as representative examples among widely used tree-based and deep learning models
[
40
,
23
]
. For each method, hyperparameters are first optimized using an 80% training and 20% validation split of the available data. A final predictive model is then trained on the full sample of size
n
. The hyperparameter optimization involves
R=30
independent runs, with the corresponding search spaces and other details are provided in Section
5.4
and Section
A.2
. Our search spaces are aligned with state-of-the-art setups proposed in
[
23
,
40
]
. The R
2
score is computed on an independently generated test set of size 10,000, and is defined as

\text{R}^{2}=1-\frac{\text{sum of squared residuals}}{\text{sample variance of the responses}}.

(12)

Each experiment is repeated 100 times to ensure stability, and the results of R
2
scores are reported in Table
2
. Additionally, Figure
3
displays the histograms of the optimized hyperparameter values of
M
and the selected architectures for
n=450
, aggregated over the 100 trials.

10
),
n=150

Model (

),

scores computed over 100 independent evaluations.

Insights from Table
2
:
Let us summarize the advantages of the boosted 01Neuro given results from Table
2
. First, the results for Model (
11
) demonstrate the universal approximation ability of boosted 01Neuro, aligning with Theorem
2
. Our model outperforms other tree-based methods on this model, which includes a multiplicative interaction term often referred to as an XOR component. The XOR structure is a standard benchmark for testing a model’s capacity to learn complex interactions
[
21
]
, and it is well known that orthogonal decision trees have difficulty capturing such patterns
[
25
]
. Second, the proposed model exhibits low sensitivity to noisy features, as evidenced in Table
2
, where the boosted 01Neuro performs comparably to other tree-based models even when
p\approx n
. Third, the results obtained without feature normalization for 01Neuro suggest that normalization is unnecessary. Fourth, Table
2
demonstrates that the training techniques introduced in Section
5
, especially the bagging method from Section
5.3
, enhance the prediction stability of boosted 01Neuro, making it comparable to the well-established stability of Random Forests. An exception is Model (
11
) with
n=450
, where the proposed method occasionally achieves exceptionally high performance, leading to greater variability in R
2
scores.

A potential limitation of the proposed model, not apparent from Table
2
, is that boosted 01Neuro has a higher computational cost compared to other tree-based models. As noted in Section
3.1.1
, boosted 01Neuro typically requires 5 to 30 times more computational time than Random Forests and XGBoost, revealing opportunities to improve its efficiency. For simplicity, detailed runtime statistics are not reported here for the simulated data experiments. However, comprehensive runtime results based on real data are presented in Section
7
. The current implementation details of boosted 01Neuro, which does not utilize GPU acceleration, are provided in Section
3.1.1
, Section
9
, and Section
A.1
.

Insights from Figure
3
:
For Model (
11
), which includes a complex XOR component, deeper architectures (specifically architectures C, E, and F, which each contain at least three layers) are necessary for accurate prediction. Because this model does not involve many additive terms, a large value of
M
is not essential for learning. In contrast, for the linear Model (
10
), the choice of architecture is more balanced, with overly shallow networks being less favorable. Since Model (
10
) comprises several additive components, increasing
M
typically improves predictive accuracy. The histograms in Figure
3
demonstrate how boosted 01Neuro adapts to different data-generating processes based on model complexity.

Figure 3
:
Histograms of the optimized
M
and architecture over 100 trials are shown, with the six architecture types
(p_{1},\cdots,p_{5})
defined in Section
5.4
. The top plots correspond to Model (
10
) with
n=450
, the bottom to Model (
11
) with
n=450
.

Remark 1
.

We also experimented with deep learning models such as ResNet
[
26
]
, but their R
2
scores were consistently performed poorly across a range of hyperparameters, offering no meaningful comparison in our data experiments. Given the ongoing debate on the effectiveness of deep learning for tabular data with many noisy features (
p\approx n
)
[
40
,
23
]
, including these results may mislead rather than clarify. Since our aim is to highlight both the strengths and limitations of 01Neuro rather than conduct a comprehensive benchmark, we omit these deep learning models from our analysis.

7
Benchmarking Predictive Accuracy on Real Data

In this section, we highlight the predictive accuracy and stability of the boosted 01Neuro with bagging from Section
5.4
by comparing it against Random Forests
[
8
]
and XGBoost
[
11
]
. Specifically, we evaluate performance using 4 selected datasets, namely elevators, house_16H, yprop_4_1, and abalone, from OpenML
[
53
]
and the UCI Machine Learning Repository
[
39
]
, based on the R
2
goodness-of-fit measure. We use a dataset of size up to
n_{\text{dataset}}\in\{1000,5000\}
for our evaluation. If the dataset contains more than
n_{\text{dataset}}
samples, we randomly select
n_{\text{dataset}}
samples; otherwise, we use the entire dataset. Our evaluation approach follows the methodology established in
[
40
,
23
]
, which provides a state-of-the-art benchmark procedure and simulation framework.

To justify our choice of benchmark models, we note that XGBoost and Random Forests consistently demonstrate strong performance across the four tabular regression datasets studied, often outperforming both deep learning models such as ResNet
[
26
]
and SAINT
[
49
]
, as well as other tree-based methods
[
23
]
. To avoid redundancy and simplify hyperparameter tuning, we focus on these two representative models. For readers interested in state-of-the-art deep learning methods like TabPFN
[
29
]
, which can also be competitive with tree-based models, we refer to
[
40
]
.

To assess prediction performance, each R
2
score defined in (
12
) is computed by splitting the dataset into full training and test sets of equal size, with the full training sample sizes specified in Tables
3
–
4
. The full training sample is further divided into training (80%) and validation (20%) sets for hyperparameter optimization. The hyperparameter tuning procedure includes
R=100
optimization runs along with the hyperparameter spaces are given in Section
5.4
and Section
A.2
. To ensure the reliability of our results, we independently repeat each evaluation 10 times across the 4 datasets. Tables
3
–
4
present the evaluation results, with runtime statistics for
n_{\text{dataset}}=5000
reported in Table
5
.

Runtime (max, mean, min in seconds) over 10 runs for each method across datasets with training sizes as shown in Table

. Each run includes 100 hyperparameter optimization steps and final model training. Parentheses indicate feature dimensionality.

We now highlight the advantages of boosted 01Neuro based on the results in Tables
3
–
4
.
First, the overall results demonstrate that the proposed model is competitive in predictive performance relative to the two selected benchmark models. While XGBoost leads on the elevators dataset, boosted 01Neuro achieves the best performance on abalone and ranks among the top on house_16H and yprop_4_1. Second, these results were obtained without feature normalization, demonstrating the convenient applicability of 01Neuro. Third, due to the stable training techniques introduced in Section
5
, boosted 01Neuro demonstrates strong performance stability in small- and medium-sized datasets (
\leq 2500
training sample size) compared to XGBoost and Random Forests. It is worth noting that the performance gains of XGBoost on the house_16H and abalone datasets as sample size increases are primarily due to a reduced incidence of extremely poor results, indicating its potential instability in small-sample scenarios.

We now discuss a potential limitation of the proposed model. The current consensus in tabular prediction performance evaluation
[
40
]
suggests that top-tier tree-based and deep learning models offer comparable accuracy across a wide range of tabular applications. However, some models, such as CatBoost
[
43
]
, which is cited as an example in
[
40
]
, are notably easier to use due to requiring minimal hyperparameter tuning and offering efficient training times. As shown in Tables
3
to
4
, the proposed model performs comparably to the selected benchmark models on three out of the four evaluated datasets, with the exception of elevators, where XGBoost consistently outperforms the other methods. However, as shown in Table
5
, Boosted 01Neuro requires approximately 5 to 30 times more training time than the slower of Random Forests and XGBoost, highlighting the need for improvements in algorithmic design or implementation efficiency.

8
Discussion

We propose a new deep learning model, 01Neuro, based on indicator activation neurons. Its boosted variant exhibits two key statistical properties: sure convergence and constructive universal approximation. Unlike most universal approximation results that are independent of training procedures, our guarantees are directly linked to the model’s explicit construction and optimization algorithm.

Three promising directions for future research based on 01Neuro are as follows. First, providing a theoretical characterization of its approximation convergence rate and extending the framework to establish high-dimensional consistency, where the number of features grows with the sample size, would deepen our understanding of the model. Second, developing more efficient optimization algorithms is essential to overcome the current computational bottleneck and make the model practical for large-scale applications. Third, applying 01Neuro to time series data for signal extraction, feature representation, and transfer learning offers a promising direction for future research.

9
Reproducibility and Code Availability

The real datasets used in Section
7
are publicly available from OpenML
[
53
]
and the UCI Machine Learning Repository
[
39
]
. To maintain anonymity during peer review, the Python code is not publicly available but can be provided upon request.

A.2
Hyperparameter spaces

We use the Python package
hyperopt
from
https://hyperopt.github.io/hyperopt/
for tuning all predictive models. The tuning procedure is introduced as follows. The full dataset is split into three subsamples, representing the training and validation sets, which constitute 80% and 20% of the full dataset, respectively. To determine the optimal hyperparameters for our predictive models, we assess
R\in\{30,100\}
distinct sets. Utilizing the Python package
hyperopt
, we perform hyperparameter sampling in each round. The training set is used to train a model based on the sampled hyperparameters. Subsequently, the model’s performance is evaluated on the validation set to score each set of hyperparameters. After conducting this process for
R
rounds, we select the set with the highest evaluation scores as the best hyperparameters. A final predictive model is then trained using this optimal set, taking into account the full dataset. The hyperparameter spaces of boosted 01Neuro, Random Forests, XGBoost are respectively given in Table
1
and Tables
7
–
8
.

Parameter space

\textnormal{Uniform}[0,1]

integer

\{1,\cdots,20\}

integer

\{2,\cdots,20\}

non-negative real number

\textnormal{Uniform}\{0,0.01,0.02,0.05\}

non-negative integer

Uniform
\{5,10,20,50,\infty\}

A.3
Training Complexity of 01Neuro

We briefly discuss the implementation and computational cost of Exploit-Explore Optimizing introduced in Section
3.1
in this section. In light of (
4
), the optimization (
5
) can be represented by a maximization problem of

g^{\text{new}}=\argmax_{g\in\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{K+1}\}}\sum_{l=0}^{1}\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\times\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=l)\}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=l)\}}}\right)^{2}.

(A.1)

This optimization problem can be solved by using tensor (i.e., multidimensional array) computing as follows.

1)
Generate
\{g_{1},\cdots,g_{K+1}\}
by sampling according to the procedure in (
5
).

2)
Perform a forward pass to compute the
n\times(K+1)
binary matrix
M_{X}
defined as

M_{X}=\begin{bmatrix}g_{1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{1})&\cdots&g_{K+1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{1})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
g_{1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})&\cdots&g_{K+1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})\end{bmatrix}.

Let
f_{L}
be equivalently denoted by
f_{L,1}
; throughout what follows, the superscript
(k)
indicates that the weights and biases associated with
g_{k}
are being used. In Step 2), for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,K+1\}
and each
h\in\{1,\cdots,p_{l}\}
at each layer
l\in\{1,\cdots,L\}
, we compute the binary outputs
f_{l,h}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}),\cdots,f_{l,h}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{X}_{n})
by evaluating
W_{l,h}^{(k)}\odot M_{X,l-1}^{(k)}>C_{l,h}^{(k)}
, where
\odot
means element-wise product,
M_{X,l-1}^{(k)}=(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}_{l-1}^{(k)}(\boldsymbol{X}_{1}),\cdots,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern is the previous layer’s output matrix,
W_{l,h}^{(k)}=(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}_{l,h}^{(k)},\cdots,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}_{l,h}^{(k)})^{{}^{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p_{l-1}}
repeats the weight vector, and
C_{l,h}^{(k)}=(c_{l,h}^{(k)},\cdots,c_{l,h}^{(k)})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
repeats the threshold. The evaluation can be broadcasted across all
(h,k)
without explicit iteration. This computation leverages element-wise products and broadcasting for efficiency.

3)
Compute the column-wise sum of
M_{X}
to obtain vector
A_{1}
, and set
A_{0}=(n,\cdots,n)-A_{1}
.

4)
Compute
S_{1}=(U_{1},\cdots,U_{n})\times M_{X}
, and set
S_{0}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i},\cdots,\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-S_{1}
.

5)
Complete the optimization in (
A.1
) by computing

\frac{S_{l}(k)}{\sqrt{A_{l}(k)}}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\times\boldsymbol{1}\{g_{k}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=l\}}{\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}\{g_{k}(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=l\}}}
for each index
k\in\{1,\cdots,K+1\}
and
l\in\{0,1\}
,
where
S_{l}(k)
and
A_{l}(k)
denote the
k
th elements of
S_{l}
and
A_{l}
, respectively. These computations are vectorized using
S_{0},S_{1},A_{0},A_{1}
.

Remark 2
.

Tensor computing involves operations on multi-dimensional arrays (tensors), encompassing linear algebra tasks such as matrix multiplication, element-wise operations, and broadcasting. Broadcasting specifically refers to the automatic expansion of array dimensions to enable arithmetic operations between tensors of different shapes.

The number of basic operations such as additions, multiplications, and comparisons required to complete a single optimization of (
A.1
) is approximately linear in
nK\left(\sum_{l=1}^{L}p_{l}p_{l-1}\right)
when
n
is large, as detailed below. The cost of sampling in Step 1 above and broadcasting operations is ignored in this calculation. Step 2) involves evaluating each neuron
h\in\{1,\cdots,p_{l}\}
at layer
l\in\{1,\cdots,L\}
for each
k\in\{1,\cdots,K+1\}
, and it takes
p_{l-1}n
multiplications and
n
comparisons per neuron. Summing over all layers, this results in a total of

(K+1)\sum_{l=1}^{L}p_{l}(p_{l-1}n+n)

basic operations. Step 3) requires
n(K+1)
additions to compute the column-wise sums. Subtractions are treated as additions in the operation count. Step 4) involves
n(K+1)
multiplications and an additional
n(K+1)
additions. Step 5) requires
2(K+1)
square root evaluations,
2(K+1)
divisions, and
K+1
comparisons to compute the final
\arg\max
.

Combining all contributions, the total number of basic operations in a single optimization of (
A.1
) is

3n(K+1)+6(K+1)+(K+1)\sum_{l=1}^{L}p_{l}(p_{l-1}n+n)
. This total is linearly proportional to
nK(\sum_{l=1}^{L}p_{l}p_{l-1})
when
n
is large, as the remaining terms are of lower order. Additionally, as discussed in Section
3.1.1
, there are at most
\sum_{l=1}^{L}w_{0}^{L-l}
active neuron units to be updated in each round. The overall operations required in one round of updating is linearly proportional to
\sum_{l=1}^{L}w_{0}^{L-l}\times nK(\sum_{l=1}^{L}p_{l}p_{l-1})
. As a result, the training complexity of the boosted 01Neuro over a single round of update is
M\sum_{l=1}^{L}w_{0}^{L-l}\times nK(\sum_{l=1}^{L}p_{l}p_{l-1})
.

Proofs of Theorems

, the definition of each

, and the finiteness of

and

, the probability of the event

is positively bounded away from zero by some constant
\varepsilon>0
that is independent of
b
, the current configuration of active units in
\boldsymbol{1}\{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern
