2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})>c\}
, and the indices of idle neurons. This lower bound depends on the input sample, which is conditioned on and thus treated as fixed. In particular, for each
b\geq 1
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{b}\mid\mathcal{X}_{n},\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}},\cdots,\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{b-1}^{c}})\geq\varepsilon,

where
\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{0}^{c}}
is defined to be zero.
As a result,

\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcup_{l=1}^{b}\mathcal{B}_{l}\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}\right)&=1-\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{l=1}^{b}\mathcal{B}_{l}^{c}\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}\right)\\
&=1-\mathbb{E}[\prod_{l=1}^{b-1}\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{l}^{c}}\times\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{b}^{c}}|\mathcal{X}_{n},\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}^{c}},\cdots,\boldsymbol{1}_{\mathcal{B}_{b-1}^{c}})\mid\mathcal{X}_{n}]\\
&\geq\cdots\\
&\geq 1-(1-\epsilon)^{b}.\end{split}

(B.1)

Now, let us turn to the output layer. On the event
\mathcal{B}_{b}
, regardless of the current status of active units, Lemma
1
guarantees a positive probability of sampling the optimal pair of weights and bias for the
(L,1)
th output neuron at the
(b+1)
th update round. In this context, the outputs of the
(L-1)
th layer are regarded as input vectors for the application of Lemma
1
. Once this optimal pair is sampled, Exploit-Explore Optimizing ensures that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(U_{i}-F(g^{(b+1)},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)^{2}=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(U_{i}-F(g,\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right).

Moreover, since each optimization step includes the current model as a candidate (i.e.,
g_{K+1}
in (
5
)), it follows that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(U_{i}-F(g^{(b_{1})},\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)^{2}=\inf_{g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(U_{i}-F(g,\boldsymbol{X}_{i})\right)

for every
b_{1}>b
.

With (
B.1
) and the arguments above, we have completed the proof of Theorem
1
.

B.2
Proof of Theorem
2

The proof of Theorem
2
closely follows that of Theorem 1 in
[
52
]
, but with a major distinction stemming from its application to boosting prediction. We provide a self-contained proof below, employing only auxiliary lemmas from
[
52
]
.

By (
7
) and recursively applying (
8
), we derive that

\begin{split}m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})&=m(\boldsymbol{X})-\gamma\sum_{q=1}^{s}\sum_{l=0}^{1}\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g_{q}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})=l\}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g_{q}(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}}\times\mathbb{E}(\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g_{q}(\boldsymbol{X})=l\}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g_{q}(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}}m_{q-1}(\boldsymbol{X}))\\
&=m(\boldsymbol{X})-\widehat{m}_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}),\end{split}

(B.2)

and for
s>q>0
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}&=\mathbb{E}(m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}+\gamma^{2}\times\mathcal{P}(g_{s},m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X}))-2\gamma\times\mathcal{P}(g_{s},m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X}))\\
&=\mathbb{E}(m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}-\sum_{l=q+1}^{s}\gamma(2-\gamma)\mathcal{P}(g_{l},m_{l-1}(\boldsymbol{X}))\\
&=\mathbb{E}(m(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}-\gamma(2-\gamma)\sum_{l=1}^{s}\mathcal{P}(g_{l},m_{l-1}(\boldsymbol{X})).\end{split}

(B.3)

With (
B.3
), we deduce that

\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{P}(g_{q},m_{q-1}(\boldsymbol{X}))\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(m(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}}{\gamma(2-\gamma)}<\infty.

(B.4)

Additionally, a direct calculation shows that for every
s>q
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})-m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}&=\mathbb{E}(m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}-\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}-2\mathbb{E}[(m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X})-m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})].\end{split}

(B.5)

Using (
B.4
)–(
B.5
) and Lemma 2.4 of
[
52
]
, it suffices to show that the absolute value of the second term in (
B.5
) tends to zero as (
B.7
) below, in order to conclude that
m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})
converges in the
L_{2}
-norm (as in (
B.8
) below).

For each
i>0
and each
l\in\{0,1\}
, denote
a_{i}(l)=|\mathbb{E}(\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g_{i}(\boldsymbol{X})=l\}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}}m_{i-1}(\boldsymbol{X}))|
. In light of (
B.2
), we deduce that for every
s>q
,

\begin{split}&|\mathbb{E}[(m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X})-m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})]|\\
&\leq\gamma\sum_{i=q+1}^{s}\sum_{l=0}^{1}\frac{|\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{1}\{g_{i}(\boldsymbol{X})=l\})|}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g_{i}(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}}\times a_{i}(l)\\
&\leq\gamma\sum_{i=q+1}^{s}\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(g_{i},m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))}\sum_{l=0}^{1}a_{i}(l)\\
&\leq\gamma\sum_{i=q+1}^{s}\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(g_{s+1},m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))t^{-1}}\sum_{l=0}^{1}a_{i}(l)\\
&\leq\gamma\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(g_{s+1},m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))t^{-1}}\sum_{i=q+1}^{s}\sqrt{2\mathcal{P}(g_{i},m_{i-1}(\boldsymbol{X}))}.\end{split}

(B.6)

The second inequality holds because

\frac{|\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X})=l\})|}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}}\leq\sqrt{\sum_{l=0}^{1}[\frac{|\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X})=l\})|}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}}]^{2}}=\sqrt{\mathcal{P}(g,m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))}

for each
g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
. The third inequality follows from that the definition of
g_{s+1}
and (
8
). The fourth inequlaity results from the definition of
a_{i}(l)
and that

\begin{split}\left(\sum_{l=0}^{1}a_{i}(l)\right)^{2}\leq 2\sum_{l=0}^{1}\left(a_{i}(l)\right)^{2}=2\mathcal{P}(g_{i},m_{i-1}(\boldsymbol{X})).\end{split}

By (
B.4
), Lemma 2.3 of
[
52
]
, and (
B.6
), we conclude that

\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\max_{q<s}\left|\mathbb{E}[(m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X})-m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})]\right|=0.

(B.7)

Furthermore, from (
B.3
)–(
B.4
) and the Monotone Convergence Theorem for sequences of real numbers, it follows that
\mathbb{E}[(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}]
converges. Combining the result of (
B.7
), the convergence of
\mathbb{E}[(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}]
, (
B.5
), and Lemma 2.4 from
[
52
]
, we conclude that
m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})
converges in the
L_{2}
-norm.

Next, we prove by contradiction that
m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})
converges to the zero function with
\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}=0
. By Lemma
3
and the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, both
m(\boldsymbol{X})
and each
m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})
lie, by construction, in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. In light of the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, we conclude that

\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})-\nu(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}=0

(B.8)

for some measurable function
\nu:\mathbb{R}^{p}\longmapsto\mathbb{R}
in
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
. In what follows, we consider the nontrivial case where
\mathbb{E}[\nu(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}>0
.

For some
\delta>0
, some
g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
, and some
l\in\{0,1\}
, it holds that

|\mathbb{E}(\nu(\boldsymbol{X})\times\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X})=l\}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}})|\geq 2\delta.

(B.9)

For if not, then
\mathbb{E}(\nu(\boldsymbol{X})\times\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X})=l\}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}})=0
for every
g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
and each
l\in\{0,1\}
, implying that
\mathbb{E}[f_{m}(\boldsymbol{X})-\nu(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}=\mathbb{E}[f_{m}(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}+\mathbb{E}[\nu(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}\geq\mathbb{E}[\nu(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}>0
for every
f_{m}\in\mathcal{D}
. Such a result contradicts the fact that
\nu(\boldsymbol{X})\in\overline{\mathcal{D}}
, i.e., there exist
f_{m}\in\mathcal{D}
with
\mathbb{E}[(f_{m}(\boldsymbol{X})-\nu(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}]\to 0
.

By (
B.8
)–(
B.9
) and the definitions of
\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
, there exists
N_{0}>0
,
l\in\{0,1\}
, and
g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}
such that
|\mathbb{E}(\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X})=l\}}{\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(g(\boldsymbol{X})=l)}}m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X}))|\geq\delta
for all
q>N_{0}
. Therefore,
for all
q>N_{0}
,

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}}\mathcal{P}(g,m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X}))\geq\delta^{2}.

(B.10)

By the definition of
g_{s}
’s in (
8
), (
B.3
), and (
B.10
), we derive that for each
s>q>N_{0}
,

\begin{split}\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}&\leq\mathbb{E}(m_{q}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}-\gamma(2-\gamma)\delta^{2}\sum_{i>q}^{s}t^{-2}.\end{split}

(B.11)

This result contradicts the non-negativity of
\mathbb{E}[m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})]^{2}\geq 0
. Hence, we conclude the desired result that
\lim_{s\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}(m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}=0
.

By this result and (
B.2
), we have completed the proof of Theorem
2
.

is measurable with positive surface measure on the

-dimensional unit sphere, and (2)

) and
|c^{{}^{\prime}}-(c+\frac{1}{2}\delta)|<\frac{\delta}{4}
. The second and third inequalities result from the definition of

Next, note that the above arguments applies to each distinct sample separation, and that there are at most
2^{\texttt{\#}\mathcal{N}}
distinct ways to separate
\mathcal{N}
into two subsamples by hyperplanes. For each separation, we can pick up a representative split. Eventually, we collect a finite set of representative splits, denoted by
\{(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern for some integer

. Furthermore, each of these splits correspond to a measure lower bound

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle w\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern . As there are finitely many distinct separations, we conclude that

, we have completed the proof of Lemma

.

&\quad\times\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern (C.1)

which, in combination with
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\frac{\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=l\}}{\sqrt{n^{-1}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{q})=l\}}}\big)^{2}=1
for
l\in\{0,1\}
and
\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=0\}\times\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{i})=1\}=0
, concludes that

\begin{split}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(F_{s}(g,\boldsymbol{X}_{i}))^{2}&=\sum_{l=0}^{1}\left(\frac{n^{-1}\sum_{q=1}^{n}m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{q})\times\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{q})=l\}}{\sqrt{n^{-1}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{q})=l\}}}\right)^{2}.\end{split}

(C.2)

The results in (
C.1
)–(
C.2
) imply that, in the update rule (
2
),

\begin{split}g_{s}&=\argmax_{g\in\mathcal{I}_{L,w_{0}}}\sum_{l=0}^{1}\left(\frac{n^{-1}\sum_{q=1}^{n}m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X}_{q})\times\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{q})=l\}}{\sqrt{n^{-1}\sum_{q=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{1}\{g(\boldsymbol{X}_{q})=l\}}}\right)^{2}.\end{split}

(C.3)

According to (
C.1
) and (
C.3
) and the definition of the infinite-sample boosted 01Neuro, which has been introduced in Section
4
, the infinite-sample analogue of (
2
) is given by

\begin{split}F_{s}(g,\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern m_{s}(\boldsymbol{X})&=m_{s-1}(\boldsymbol{X})-\gamma F_{s}(g_{s},\boldsymbol{X}),\end{split}

(C.4)

which concludes (

C.3
Proof of Lemma
3

We begin the proof by demonstrating that the class
\mathcal{D}
includes all linear combinations of finitely many rectangular indicator functions, where each indicator function depends on only
k
coordinates. Note that it suffices to show that an 01Neuro with configuration
w_{0}=2
and
L=2+\lceil\log_{2}k\rceil
can approximate any rectangular indicator function depending on
k
-coordinates. Specifically, for any rectangle
R^{\prime}=[a_{1},b_{1}]\times\cdots\times[a_{k},b_{k}]\subset[0,1]^{k}
, any index set
\{i_{1},\cdots,i_{k}\}\subset\{1,\cdots,p\}
, and the corresponding set

R=\left\{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}\in[0,1]^{p}:(x_{i_{1}},\cdots,x_{i_{k}})\in R^{\prime}\right\},
we construct a 01Neuro with
w_{0}=2
and
L=2+\lceil\log_{2}k\rceil
as follows. The following construction adheres to the Exploit-Explore Optimizing method introduced in Section
3.1
, including the use of
w_{0}
-sparse unit-length weight vectors and specific bias sampling rules.

a_{3}=\lceil\frac{k}{2}\rceil=\lceil\frac{a_{2}}{2}\rceil
active units at the third layer. The
l
th layer, for
l>3
, is defined in a manner analogous to the third layer, implying that there are at most
a_{l}=\lceil\frac{a_{l-1}}{2}\rceil
active units at layer
l
.

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}\in R\}\Big|d\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}=0.

Such a results and the definition of
\mathcal{D}
conclude that
\mathcal{D}
includes all linear combinations of finitely many rectangular indicator functions, where each indicator function depends on only
k
coordinates.

On the other hand, it is well known that
p
-dimensional step functions, defined as linear combinations of finitely many rectangular indicator functions, each depending on at most
p
coordinates, can approximate any measurable function on a compact domain, such as
[0,1]^{p}
, in the Lebesgue measure sense
[
50
,
13
]
. However, since our focus is on the distribution of
\boldsymbol{X}
rather than the Lebesgue measure, we assume in Condition
2
that
\boldsymbol{X}
has a bounded probability density function to apply existing results. Under this assumption, there exists a constants
C_{2}>0
such that for any measurable function
f:[0,1]^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\mathbb{E}[f^{2}(\boldsymbol{X})]\leq C_{2}\int_{[0,1]^{p}}f^{2}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}})\,d\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle x\hfil$\crcr}}}.

This equivalence between
L_{2}
-norms under the probability measure and the Lebesgue measure allows us to apply standard approximation results
[
13
,
50
]
.

In particular, by arguments similar to those in Proposition 3.4.3 of
[
13
]
and Theorem 4.3 of
[
50
]
, the class
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
contains
\mathcal{L}(k)
. That is, for every
m\in\mathcal{L}(k)
, there exists a sequence
f_{i}\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\big[(m(\boldsymbol{X})-f_{i}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}\big]=0
. Here, we highlight two extensions in our setting in comparison to the similar results presented in
[
50
,
13
]
: first, we consider the distribution of
\boldsymbol{X}
instead of the Lebesgue measure, and second, we account for
k
-sparse measurable functions in
\mathcal{L}(k)
. Nevertheless, with Condition
2
, the standard arguments in the cited references apply directly, and the detailed proof is omitted for simplicity.

To finish the proof of Lemma
3
, note that by the previous results it holds that for each
m^{(r)}\in\mathcal{L}(k)
, there exists
f_{i}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\mathbb{E}\big[(m^{(r)}(\boldsymbol{X})-f_{i}^{(r)}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}\big]
approaches zero. By this result and an application of the Minkowski inequality, it holds that

\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\{[\sum_{r=1}^{R_{0}}m^{(r)}(\boldsymbol{X})]-\sum_{r=1}^{R_{0}}f_{i}^{(r)}(\boldsymbol{X})\}^{2}}\leq\sum_{r=1}^{R_{0}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\big[(m^{(r)}(\boldsymbol{X})-f_{i}^{(r)}(\boldsymbol{X}))^{2}\big]}\rightarrow 0\textnormal{ as }i\rightarrow\infty,

which, in combination with the facts that
\sum_{r=1}^{R_{0}}m^{(r)}\in\mathcal{L}(k,R_{0})
and
\sum_{r=1}^{R_{0}}f_{i}^{(r)}\in\mathcal{D}
, concludes the desired results that
\overline{\mathcal{D}}
contains
\mathcal{L}(k,R_{0})
. We have completed the proof of Lemma
3
.
